WHICH RIG PRINTED WHAT. The rig as committed at 6fb53e4 printed R1 to
X-cl-633 (R1c: as at 8fa4998). The fixes committed in f9ca702 landed
between runs: cache.py's model reading (F-HARNESS-134) from X-cl-645 on,
so X-cl-633's `model/build.mjs ... code C1+C2: POISONED` lines are the
defect it fixed, not a poisoned unit (its final cache, read again with
the fix, holds none); rig.qml.in's guide empty-state marks and
analyze.py's no-service view lines from X-u-650 on; the views' verdict
beside a service from before the stamps from B-man-0 on; the
unattached-view naming and mode t's restart from T-2 on; RIG_C2_PART
the P-logos runs; the
reading-threw verdict was written after M-text-3 ran, so M-text-3's
`agree` lines below read THE NOTICE DOES NOT FIT where f9ca702 reads that
the reading threw and the notice fits, and T-1's four enable marks read
DOES NOT FIT for the bar its resolve timer had not yet handed the
service. Every arm was read again with f9ca702's analyze.py over its kept
files: 329 `agree` lines in 47 arms, all fitting (R1c's rig carries no
stamps and gives none).

What is printed below is each run's stdout and stderr as written, the
wrapper's `# run` lines with the worktree and scratch paths written
<worktree> and <scratch>, but for lines dropped by the 2026-11-24 RESULT
files' filters, named here so nothing is dropped silently: the cage's own
warnings, which every shell of every arm prints at every start
(MESA-EGL, the host portal, the Hyprland signature, layershell,
wl_egl_window, eglSwapBuffers; their `log N notable line(s)` counts
include them); the plugin's two start-up notes, the config shield's mode
change and the bar entry's migration, which every fresh install prints
once; and cache.py's lines for files whose text is the same in C1, C2 and
C3 and whose unit agrees (Model.mjs and the model's parts but build.mjs),
never a POISONED or stale line. The middle dot of a guide footer is
written \xb7. A "read" near a merge may be git's own open (the open watch
sees every open); the times mix two clocks (watch.py's read stamps and
tmpfs mtimes in 1 ms ticks), so a comparison across them under about 2 ms
is not one; the verdicts rest on the marks, the `build` replies and the
cache files' contents.
